The **Sweden Democrats** ([Swedish](26689.md): *Sverigedemokraterna* [\[ˈsvæ̂rjɛdɛmʊˌkrɑːtɛɳa\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Swedish) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/0/0a/Sv-Sverigedemokraterna.ogg/Sv-Sverigedemokraterna.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/0/0a/Sv-Sverigedemokraterna.ogg/Sv-Sverigedemokraterna.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>, **SD** [\[ˈɛ̂sːdeː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Swedish) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/a/aa/Sv-S.D.ogg/Sv-S.D.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/a/aa/Sv-S.D.ogg/Sv-S.D.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>) is a [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism)[^cite_note-StarkeKaasch2013-6][^cite_note-7] and [right-wing populist](8354382.md)[^cite_note-Rydgren-8][^cite_note-2013Berezin255-9][^cite_note-10] political party in [Sweden](5058739.md) founded in 1988.[^cite_note-StarkeKaasch2013-6][^cite_note-Rydgren-8][^cite_note-2013Berezin255-9] In 2023, it was the largest member of Sweden's [right-wing](56522.md) bloc and the second-largest party in the [Riksdag](158651.md). It provides [confidence and supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence_and_supply) to the right-wing ruling coalition [Kristersson cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristersson_cabinet).[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] Within the [European Union](9317.md), the party is a member of the [European Conservatives and Reformists Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Conservatives_and_Reformists_Party).[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-Independent2-14]

The party describes itself as [social conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism) with a nationalist foundation.[^cite_note-svd-15][^cite_note-party_platform-16][^cite_note-17] The party has also been variously characterised by universities, political commentators, and international media as [economic nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_nationalism),[^cite_note-18] [national-conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservatism),[^cite_note-Rydgren-8][^cite_note-StarkeKaasch2013-6] [anti-immigration](6491626.md),[^cite_note-21] [anti-Islam](162173.md),[^cite_note-22] [anti-gender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-gender_movement_in_Sweden),[^cite_note-Karlberg-23] [Eurosceptic](10037.md),[^cite_note-29] and [right-wing](56522.md)[^cite_note-Toemmel2014-30] or [far-right](221220.md).[^cite_note-Independent2-14][^cite_note-31] The Sweden Democrats reject the far-right label, saying that it no longer represents its political beliefs.[^cite_note-\:42-32][^cite_note-\:10-33][^cite_note-\:11-34] Among the party's founders, original leadership and early members were several people that had previously been active in [white nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism) and [neo-Nazi](54361.md) political parties and organizations.[^cite_note-r1089-35][^cite_note-kvallsposten-36][^cite_note-ekstrom-37][^cite_note-telegraphboots-38][^cite_note-Telegraph2-39] Under the leadership of [Jimmie Åkesson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmie_%C3%85kesson) since 2005, the SD underwent a process of reform by expelling hardline members and moderating its beliefs, building on a work that had begun during the late 1990s and early 2000s when the party began to distance itself from extreme-right politics and sought to position its image as a more mainstream party.[^cite_note-Rydgren-8][^cite_note-telegraphboots-38] Today, the SD officially rejects fascism and [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) on their platform and since 2012 has maintained a zero-tolerance policy against "extremists", "lawbreakers", and "racists".[^cite_note-zero-tolerance_policy-40] In 2025, the SD issued an official apology for its previous links to neo-Nazism during its early years.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42]

The Sweden Democrats oppose current [Swedish immigration](24537353.md) and integration policies, instead supporting stronger restrictions on immigration and measures for immigrants to assimilate into Swedish culture. The Sweden Democrats are critical of [multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism) and support having a common national and cultural identity, which they believe improves social cohesion. The party supports the [Swedish welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_in_Sweden) but is against providing welfare to people who are not Swedish citizens and permanent residents of Sweden. The Sweden Democrats support a [mixed market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) combining ideas from the centre-left and centre-right. The party supports [same-sex marriage](92656.md), [civil unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_union) for gay couples, and [gender-affirming surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender-affirming_surgery) but prefers that children be raised in a traditional [nuclear family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_family) and argues that churches or private institutions should have the final say on performing a wedding over the state. The SD also calls for a ban on forced, polygamous or child marriages and stricter enforcement of laws against honour violence. The Sweden Democrats support keeping [Sweden's nuclear power plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_Sweden) in order to [mitigate climate change](2119179.md) but argues that other countries should reduce their [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md) instead of Sweden, which the party believes is doing enough to reduce their emissions. The Sweden Democrats support generally increasing minimum sentences for crimes, as well as increasing [police](47359232.md) resources and personnel. The party also supports increasing the number of [Swedish Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Army) brigades and supports raising Sweden's defense spending.

Support for the Sweden Democrats has grown steadily since the 1990s and the party crossed the 4% threshold necessary for parliamentary representation for the first time during the [2010 Swedish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Swedish_general_election), polling 5.7% and gaining 20 seats in the [Riksdag](158651.md).[^cite_note-autogenerated1-43][^cite_note-autogenerated2-44] This increase in popularity has been compared by international media to other similar anti-immigration movements in Europe.[^cite_note-Castle20092010-45] The party received increased support in the [2018 Swedish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Swedish_general_election), when it polled 17.5% and secured 62 seats in parliament, becoming the third largest party in Sweden.[^cite_note-parl2014-46][^cite_note-47] The Sweden Democrats were formerly isolated in the Riksdag until the late 2010s, with other parties maintaining [a policy of refusing cooperation with them](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cordon_sanitaire_\(politics\)).[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-Reuterspoll-49] In 2019, the leader of the [Christian Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democrats_\(Sweden\)), [Ebba Busch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebba_Busch) announced that her party was ready to start negotiations with the Sweden Democrats in the Riksdag,[^cite_note-Hamidi-Nia-50] as did [Moderate Party](202985.md) leader [Ulf Kristersson](3246360.md). In the [2022 Swedish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Swedish_general_election), the party ran as part of a broad right-wing alliance with those two parties and the [Liberals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberals_\(Sweden\)), and came second overall with 20.5% of the vote.[^cite_note-\:6-51] Following the election and the [Tidö Agreement](72007581.md), it was negotiated that SD agreed to support [a Moderate Party-led government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristersson_cabinet) together with the Christian Democrats and the Liberals.[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] It is the first time that SD holds direct influence over the government.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56]

## History

### Early years (1988–1995)



Early sticker used by the Sweden Democrats with the slogan *Bevara Sverige Svenskt* ("Keep Sweden Swedish")

The Sweden Democrats party was founded in 1988 as a direct successor to the [Sweden Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweden_Party),[^cite_note-Rydgren-8] which in turn had been formed in 1986 by the merger of the activist group *[Bevara Sverige Svenskt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bevara_Sverige_Svenskt)* (BSS; in English: "Keep Sweden Swedish") and a faction of the [Swedish Progress Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progress_Party_\(Sweden\)). The SD continued to use Keep Sweden Swedish as its slogan until the late 1990s.[^cite_note-r1089-35] The SD claims 6 February 1988 as the date of its foundation and that the party was formally registered after a meeting in Stockholm designed to bring together various nationalist movements who issued a [white paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_paper) for a new party, although observers tend to see the party's foundation as part of a complex decade-long series of events, with some even calling into question whether a meeting took place.[^cite_note-57]

Initially, the SD did not have a single centralized leader and was instead fronted by two alternating spokespeople before [Anders Klarström](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anders_Klarstr%C3%B6m) became the party's first official chairman and head of the Sweden Democrats' national board in 1989.[^cite_note-ekstrom-37][^cite_note-58]

According to the anti-racism publication *[Expo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expo_\(magazine\))* and political historians Duncan McDonnell and Tony Gustafsson, it is generally agreed that the Sweden Democrats have never been a fascist or a [neo-Nazi](54361.md) political party,[^cite_note-Expo_SD_Historia-59][^cite_note-60] although studies by *Expo* documented that around 60% of the SD's founders and early members had previously belonged to both Swedish and foreign neo-fascist and white nationalist groups. The party also had a reputation in the late 1980s and early 1990s for attracting [skinhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skinhead) gangs to its public events.[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-kvallsposten-36] The Expo study also claimed that nine of the original 30 people who founded the SD had associations to known Nordic fascist parties such as the [New Swedish Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Swedish_Movement) and the [neo-Nazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism_in_Sweden) [Nordic Realm Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Realm_Party) (*Nordiska rikspartiet*, abbr. *NRP*) while another eighteen had been part of the [Keep Sweden Swedish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keep_Sweden_Swedish) organization. The study estimated that around sixty percent of party's national board members between 1989 and 1995 were connected to neo-Nazi movements in various ways both before and during their time in the party.[^cite_note-Expo_SD_Historia-59] However, the study also noted that a majority of these members were no longer active within the party by the mid-to-late-1990s.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-Mattsson-63] The party's first auditor, [Gustaf Ekström](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustaf_Ekstr%C3%B6m), was a *[Waffen-SS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waffen-SS)* veteran and had been a member of the national socialist party *[Svensk Socialistisk Samling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Workers'_Party_\(Sweden\))* in the 1940s.[^cite_note-edoc.hu-berlin.de-64] The SD's first chairman Anders Klarström and party co-founders and first board members Fritz Håkansson and Gösta Bergqvist had all been members of the Nordic Realm Party.[^cite_note-Schön-65] Klarström later elaborated he had briefly been part of the NRP as a teenager before distancing himself from it by the time he became SD leader.[^cite_note-71] The first version of the SD's old youth-wing, the *[Sweden Democratic Youth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweden_Democratic_Youth)* was accused of having dual leadership with neo-Nazi youth movements until 1995[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] while the SD's logo from the 1990s until 2006 was a version of the torch used by the British [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_\(UK\)).[^cite_note-76] The SD also encountered controversy for some of its early policy ideas before 1999, which included a proposal to repatriate most immigrants who came to Sweden from 1970, banning adoption of foreign born children and reinstating the death penalty.[^cite_note-auto6-77]

The party promoted concerts by the Swedish offshoot of [Rock Against Communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Against_Communism) and sponsored music of the nationalist [Viking rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking_rock) band [Ultima Thule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultima_Thule_\(Swedish_band\)). Various party officials today acknowledge that being fans of Ultima Thule's music factored prominently in their decision to become politically engaged.[^cite_note-Teitelbaum_2013-78] Early on, the party's newspaper *SD-Kuriren* promoted connections to its readers such as the [National Democratic Party of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_of_Germany), the American [National Association for the Advancement of White People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_for_the_Advancement_of_White_People) (founded by [David Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Duke)), British politician [John Tyndall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Tyndall_\(far-right_activist\)) and publications like the Nazi *[Nation Europa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_Europa)* and *[Nouvelle École](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nouvelle_%C3%89cole)*, a newspaper that advocates [racial biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_racism) and the British neo-Nazi [Combat 18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combat_18) movement.[^cite_note-vitbok-79][^cite_note-SDBulle_11-89-80]

The SD won municipal representation for the first time during the [1991 Swedish local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Swedish_local_elections) in [Dals-Ed Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dals-Ed_Municipality) and [Höör](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B6%C3%B6r).[^cite_note-SD10-81]

### Moderation and growth (1995–2010)



[Jimmie Åkesson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmie_%C3%85kesson), interviewed before an SVT party-leader debate in 2014

In 1995, Klarström was replaced as SD chairman by [Mikael Jansson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikael_Jansson_\(politician\)), a former member of the [Centre Party](217370.md). Jansson strove to make the party more respectable and took a direct stance against displays of extremism within its ranks. In 1995, the SD closed down its youth-wing and after skinheads started to impose on SD meetings, consuming alcohol at party events, displaying fascist imagery and the wearing of any kind of political uniform were formally banned in 1996. Also in 1996, it was revealed that an SD spokeswoman [Tina Hallgren-Bengtsson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tina_Hallgren?action=edit\&redlink=1) had been to a party meeting of [National Socialist Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Front) posing in a Nazi uniform. Opposition to the party have mistakenly mixed these two events together and falsely claim that she was wearing the uniform at a rally of the Sweden Democrats and that it was because of this that the uniform ban came about.[^cite_note-82][^cite_note-r108-83][^cite_note-nrk-84] During the early 1990s, the SD sought to become a more conventional political party and became more influenced by the French [National Rally](67119.md), as well as the [Freedom Party of Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Party_of_Austria), the [Danish People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_People's_Party), German [The Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_\(Germany\)) and Italian [National Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Alliance_\(Italy\)).[^cite_note-r109-85] SD received economic support for the [1998 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Swedish_general_election) from the then called French National Front, and became active in [Jean-Marie Le Pen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Marie_Le_Pen)'s [Euronat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euronat) from the same time.[^cite_note-nrk-84][^cite_note-expo-86]

By the end of the end of the decade, the party began to take further steps to moderate itself by distancing itself from all forms of fascist ideology, softening its policies on immigration and capital punishment, as well as removing party figures deemed to be too controversial.[^cite_note-87] In 1999, the SD left Euronat although the youth wing remained affiliated until 2002.[^cite_note-expo-86] In 2001, the most extreme faction of the SD's national board was expelled, leading to the formation of the more radical [National Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democrats_\(Sweden\)) which in turn resulted in many of the SD's remaining hardline members departing for the new party.[^cite_note-r109-85] During the [2002 Swedish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Swedish_general_election) the SD became the largest party outside of the Riksdag and won 50 seats in municipalities during the local elections held at the same time.[^cite_note-88]

During the early 2000s, a [reformist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformist) faction known as the so-called "Scania gang", also known as the "Gang of Four" or "Fantastic Four", which consisted of the youth wing chairman [Jimmie Åkesson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmie_%C3%85kesson), as well as [Björn Söder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bj%C3%B6rn_S%C3%B6der), [Mattias Karlsson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mattias_Karlsson_\(politician,_born_1977\)) and [Richard Jomshof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Jomshof) continued and expanded the moderation policy, which included ousting openly extremist members, banning neo-Nazi activists from attending party events or obtaining membership, and further revising the SD's policy platform.[^cite_note-nrk-84] Before the 2002 election, former [Member of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_\(Sweden\)) (MP) for the [Moderate Party](202985.md), [Sten Andersson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sten_Christer_Andersson) defected to SD, citing that the party had gotten rid of its extreme-right elements.[^cite_note-expo-86] In 2003, the party declared the [Universal Declaration of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Declaration_of_Human_Rights) to be a cornerstone of its policies.[^cite_note-89] In 2005, Åkesson defeated Jansson in a leadership contest. Shortly after, the party changed its logo from the flaming torch to one featuring an *[Anemone hepatica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anemone_hepatica)*, reminiscent of the party's very first, but short-lived, logo (a stylised *[Myosotis scorpioides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myosotis_scorpioides)*).[^cite_note-sdhis-90]

### Entrance into parliament and ideological realignment (2010–2014)

In the [2010 Swedish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Swedish_general_election), SD won representation in the [Swedish Riksdag](158651.md) for the first time, with 5.7% of the vote and 20 MPs.

In 2010, the SD leadership introduced a charter against racism on the party platform and later expanded this into a zero-tolerance policy regarding political extremism and law breaking. After some of the SD's elected members caused controversies during the party's first term in the Riksdag, the SD also stated it would introduce a vetting procedure for its future parliamentary candidates and issued updated guidelines on conduct and communication for party members.[^cite_note-SD_launches_an_internal_glossbook-91][^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93] In 2011, the party also changed its self-description from "nationalist" to "social conservative".[^cite_note-94] In 2012 a report by a Swedish government agency declared that the SD had made significant progress in reforming itself but still had work to do in coming to terms with its past.[^cite_note-95]

Sweden Democrat MP [William Petzäll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Petz%C3%A4ll) was persuaded to leave the party on 26 September 2011 while still retaining his parliamentary seat.[^cite_note-expressen-96] This was done because of Petzäll's [substance abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Substance_abuse) and the problems this might cause for SD's public image. Petzäll later died of an overdose and his seat was turned over to [Stellan Bojerud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellan_Bojerud) in September 2012.

In November 2012, videos from August 2010 were released, in segments, over the course of three days by Swedish newspaper *[Expressen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expressen)* (a year earlier, *Expressen* had released the same videos without making much noise). This came to be known as the [Iron pipe scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_pipe_scandal), although the same videos had already been released on [YouTube](3524766.md) by Erik Almqvist in 2010. The videos, recorded by MP [Kent Ekeroth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_Ekeroth), featured him along with fellow Sweden Democrats MP [Erik Almqvist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erik_Almqvist) and [Christian Westling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Westling?action=edit\&redlink=1). The videos show Almqvist arguing with comedian [Soran Ismail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soran_Ismail): Almqvist is referring to Sweden as "my country, not your country", as an insult to Ismail. They are also shown arguing with a drunken man. A woman can also be seen approaching Kent Ekeroth while filming; he calls her a whore and pushes her out of the way. A few minutes later they are seen picking up iron bars.[^cite_note-97] Coming only a month after party leader Åkesson had instated a zero-tolerance policy towards racism in the party,[^cite_note-zero-tolerance_policy-40] the release of the video caused Almqvist to leave his position as the party's economic policy spokesperson and his place in the executive committee on 14 November. He excused himself as having been under a lot of pressure and threats of violence at the time.[^cite_note-98] As more segments of the video were released, revealing the other two men's involvement, the party announced on 15 November that Ekeroth would take a break from his position as the party's justice policy spokesman.[^cite_note-99] Almqvist and Ekeroth both took time off from their parliament seats. [Sweden Democratic Youth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweden_Democratic_Youth) president [Gustav Kasselstrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Kasselstrand) and vice president [William Hahne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Hahne) criticised the decision to remove Almqvist and Ekeroth in an [op-ed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op-ed) in *[Dagens Nyheter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dagens_Nyheter)*, arguing that the party should not give in to media pressure.[^cite_note-100]



Sweden Democrat supporters in Stockholm during the 2014 European elections

Only two weeks after Almqvist and Ekeroth were forced to step down, fellow MP [Lars Isovaara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lars_Isovaara) reported being robbed of his backpack and pushed out of his wheelchair by "two unknown men of an immigrant background". When trying to get into the Riksdag, Isovaara was himself reported by the police for racial abuse against safety guards.[^cite_note-101] The Sweden Democrats initially defended Isovaara, but backed down when *Expressen* revealed that Isovaara had actually forgotten his backpack at a restaurant, and that the two men had helped him when he fell out of his wheelchair.[^cite_note-102] He left his seat in the Riksdag on 29 November, and was replaced by [Markus Wiechel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Markus_Wiechel).[^cite_note-103]

### Rise in national support (2014–2018)

In the [European election of 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_European_Parliament_election_in_Sweden), SD received 9.67% of votes, winning two seats in the European Parliament and becoming the fifth largest party in the country. After speculation that the SD would join an EU parliamentary group with [Marine Le Pen](572897.md) the SD stated that its preferred partners would be the [Danish People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_People's_Party) and the [UK Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Independence_Party) (UKIP) or to sit as independents.[^cite_note-104]The party later joined the [Alliance for Direct Democracy in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_for_Direct_Democracy_in_Europe) and the [Europe of Freedom and Direct Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe_of_Freedom_and_Direct_Democracy) group.

In the [2014 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Swedish_general_election), the Sweden Democrats received 12.9% of the votes, doubling their support and becoming the third-largest party. The party remained big in [Scania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scania) and [Blekinge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blekinge); for example in [Malmö](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malm%C3%B6) the party received 14% of the votes, in [Landskrona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landskrona) it received 19% of the votes and in [Sjöbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sj%C3%B6bo) a total of 30% rendering the party the largest in that municipality.[^cite_note-105] Other parties, however, remained firm in their decision to isolate them from exerting influence. Out of 29 constituencies electing parliamentarians, the party was the second largest in "Scania North & East" while being the third largest party in 25.[^cite_note-106] Although relying heavily on rural areas and the deep south, the party also made strong inroads and results above 15% in some medium-sized central Sweden cities such as [Norrköping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norrk%C3%B6ping), [Eskilstuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eskilstuna) and [Gävle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A4vle), indicating a widening of its voter base in all areas.

Some time after that, Åkesson announced he would go on sick leave due to [burnout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupational_burnout).[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108] [Mattias Karlsson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mattias_Karlsson_\(politician,_born_1977\)) was appointed to temporarily take over Åkesson's duties as party leader.

On 23 March 2015, it was announced that Åkesson would return from his leave of absence to resume his duties as party leader following an interview to be broadcast on the Friday, 27 March instalment of the *[Skavlan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skavlan)* program on [SVT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sveriges_Television), and a subsequent press conference with the Swedish media.[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110]

Amid media coverage regarding the high immigration figures and the [European migrant crisis](46415102.md), the Sweden Democrats soared in all [opinion polls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Swedish_general_election#Opinion_polls) during the summer of 2015, even topping web-based polls from [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) and Sentio in late summer, with a little over a quarter of the vote.[^cite_note-111] The party also saw rising support in phone-based polls, although the swing was lower.

### Entering mainstream politics (2018–2022)



SD rally in [Ystad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ystad) in August 2018 ahead of the 2018 Swedish general election

In early 2018, the [far-right](221220.md)[^cite_note-112] [Alternative for Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_for_Sweden) (AfS) was founded by members of the [Sweden Democratic Youth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweden_Democratic_Youth), who were collectively expelled from the Sweden Democrats in 2015.[^cite_note-113] Three Sweden Democrat members of the [Riksdag](158651.md) – [Olle Felten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olle_Felten), [Jeff Ahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Ahl) and former leader [Mikael Jansson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikael_Jansson_\(politician\)) – subsequently defected to the party.[^cite_note-114] Senior Sweden Democrats spokespeople such as [Henrik Vinge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrik_Vinge) were critical of the AfS and described it as containing people who were too extreme for SD.[^cite_note-115]

On 2 July 2018, the two Sweden Democrats MEPs left the [EFDD group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe_of_Freedom_and_Direct_Democracy) and moved to the [European Conservatives and Reformists Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Conservatives_and_Reformists_Group).

In the [2018 Swedish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Swedish_general_election), the SD increased its support to 17.5% of the vote,[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119][^cite_note-Duxbury2018-120] though it did not grow as much as most polls had predicted.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122] According to Emily Schultheis of *[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy)*, the SD won an ideological victory, as it "effectively set the terms for debate" and forced its rivals to adopt immigration policies similar to its own,[^cite_note-123] and other reporters made similar observations.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125] The SD performed particularly well in [Skåne County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sk%C3%A5ne_County), having the highest number of voters in 21 out of the county's 33 municipalities.[^cite_note-126] An SVT analysis of the results found that at least 22 seats in 17 city councils would be empty as the Sweden Democrats won more seats than the number of candidates it had.[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128] The party also received its first mayor, in [Hörby Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B6rby_Municipality).[^cite_note-129]

Following the election, [Christian Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democrats_\(Sweden\)) leader [Ebba Busch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebba_Busch) announced that her party was willing to enter negotiations with the Sweden Democrats in the Riksdag.[^cite_note-Hamidi-Nia-50] In December 2019, [Moderate Party](202985.md) leader [Ulf Kristersson](3246360.md) held an official meeting with the Sweden Democrat leadership for the first time, despite having previously ruled out negotiating with the party. This led to speculation that the SD could be included in a new centre-right grouping to replace the [Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_\(Sweden\)) which had collapsed after the [Centre Party](217370.md) and the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberals_\(Sweden\)) left to support the Social Democratic led government.[^cite_note-auto5-130][^cite_note-auto3-131]

In October 2018, the Sweden Democrats went into a governing coalitions with the [Moderate Party](202985.md) and the Christian Democrats for the first time in [Staffanstorp Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staffanstorp_Municipality), [Sölvesborg Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%B6lvesborg_Municipality), [Herrljunga Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herrljunga_Municipality) and [Bromölla Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brom%C3%B6lla_Municipality).[^cite_note-auto9-132][^cite_note-133] In Bromölla, coalition felt apart in 2020, while new coalitions with the SD emerged in [Svalöv Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sval%C3%B6v_Municipality) (2019), [Bjuv Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bjuv_Municipality) (2020) and [Surahammar Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surahammar_Municipality) (2021).

In 2020, [Mattias Karlsson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mattias_Karlsson_\(politician,_born_1977\)), the former group leader of the Sweden Democrats in the Riksdag founded [Oikos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oikos_\(Swedish_think-tank\)), a conservative think-tank which has been alleged to be an "extension of the Sweden Democrats' political project", supposedly also receiving funding from the party.[^cite_note-Poohl-134]

In 2021, the SD was invited to participate in alternative budget agreement talks with the Christian Democrats and the Moderates for the first time. That same year the SD also issued a [vote of no confidence](208101.md) against the [Löfven II cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%B6fven_II_cabinet) citing the government's handling on immigration, the economy and housing which was carried by the other opposition parties and led to Löfven's impeachment. The SD had previously issued a vote of no confidence in the government in 2015, albeit without success.[^cite_note-135]

### 2022 general election (since 2022)

Ahead of the [2022 Swedish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Swedish_general_election), the SD attempted to form a conservative grouping with the [Moderates](202985.md), Christian Democrats and the [Liberals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberals_\(Sweden\)) and requested ministerial posts in government should the right-wing bloc form a parliamentary majority.[^cite_note-136] During the election, the SD campaigned to reduce asylum migration close to zero, stricter policies on work permits, lower energy bills and a tougher stance on gang violence with longer prison sentences.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138] Preliminary results indicated that the Sweden Democrats had seen their strongest result to date and had overtaken the Moderates to become the second largest party with 20.6% of the vote. The result was confirmed after the election.[^cite_note-139]

In October 2022, the SD was allocated chairmanship of four parliamentary committees for the first time in the Riksdag with party secretary [Richard Jomshof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Jomshof) appointed to head the [Justice Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_Justice), [Aron Emilsson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aron_Emilsson) the [Committee on Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_Foreign_Affairs_\(Sweden\)), [Tobias Andersson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobias_Andersson_\(politician\)) the [Committee on Industry and Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_Industry_and_Trade_\(Sweden\)), and [Magnus Persson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnus_Persson_\(politician\)) the [Committee on the Labour Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_the_Labour_Market). SD parliamentarians were appointed as international delegation leaders for the first time, with [Markus Wiechel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Markus_Wiechel) becoming chairman of the Swedish delegation to the [Parliamentary Assembly of the Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_Assembly_of_the_Council_of_Europe), [Björn Söder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bj%C3%B6rn_S%C3%B6der) for the [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md) and [Adam Marttinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Marttinen) the chairman of the joint-parliamentary group for [Europol](525329.md).[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141]

The party also formed a deal with Moderate leader [Ulf Kristersson](3246360.md) to provide for the first time in their history parliamentary support to a Moderate Party-led government as part of the [Tidö Agreement](72007581.md).[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] In May 2023, SD representative [Elsa Widding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elsa_Widding) left the party after she was criticised for attending a conference organised by *Searchlight.* She remained as an independent politician voting with the SD group within the Tidö coalition.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145] In 2025 the SD Riksdag group dropped to 70 MPs after [Katja Nyberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katja_Nyberg_\(politician\)) and [Sara-Lena Bjälkö](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sara-Lena_Bj%C3%A4lk%C3%B6) both resigned from the party; Nyberg chose to leave the SD after receiving a drunk driving charge while Bjälkö left to join the [Christian Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democrats_\(Sweden\)).[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] In 2026 the SD group rose back up to 72 MPs after [Moderate Party](202985.md) parliamentarians [Arin Karapet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arin_Karapet) and [Marléne Lund Kopparklint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marl%C3%A9ne_Lund_Kopparklint) defected to the Sweden Democrats.[^cite_note-148]

Ahead of the [2026 Swedish general election](72049993.md), the [Liberals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberals_\(Sweden\)) announced they had eliminated all red lines on cooperating with the Sweden Democrats in a future centre-right coalition becoming the last party in the Tidö Agreement to completely end the *[cordon sanitaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cordon_sanitaire_\(politics\))* against the SD. Although both part of the Tidö coalition, the Liberals had previously opposed appointing SD politicians into cabinet roles before changing its stance in March 2026. The decision was supported by the Moderates and the Christian Democrats but it also prompted several Liberal politicians to quit the party in protest.[^cite_note-149] That same month, Prime Minister [Ulf Kristersson](3246360.md) stated that he would include the SD in the cabinet and give the party ministerial duties should the right-wing bloc gain another majority.[^cite_note-150]

In the 2026 parliamentary election, the Sweden Democrats were projected to lose around 11 seats, marking the first parliamentary election in which the party's share of the vote declined.[^cite_note-151] Åkesson subsequently blamed both the Social Democrats and parties on the left for “mobilizing the Islamist voters who previously had other parties to vote for" and the emergence of smaller right-wing parties which failed to reach the threshold to enter the Riksdag for costing the SD support while political scientist Nicholas Aylott and journalist Tim Ross argued that the party had been a "victim of its own success" in cutting immigration and crime thereby reducing the importance of issues the SD had been associated with along with Social Democrat leader [Magdalena Andersson](41178429.md) pledging a stricter stance on immigration.[^cite_note-152]
